                                      PREFACE Introduction

                                      This thesis presents an introduction to the illustration of reflected
                                      sundials in the 17th century,employing a methodology that en-
                                      compasses various layers of information, spanning from historical
                                      insights to astronomical aspects. The magnificent sundial clock at
                                      Lycée Stendhal in Grenoble, which dates from 1673, is the focus of
                                      this inquiry.

                                      MA in Architecture
                                      Thesis program 2023

The Invisible Gem of Horloge Solaire Du Lycée Stendhal
Grenoble (FR)

              Abstract

    This thesis is an introduction of representation on reflective sundials in
    the 17th century which has multiple layers of information from history to
    astronomy by applying representation methodology. The survey is about
    the magnificent sundial clock located at Lycée Stendhal in Grenoble, dat-
    ing from 1673(1). The sun clock at Grenoble is a testament to the tech-
    nological advances of the 17th century. This clock is built in fresco and
    covers 100m2 of wall and ceiling space(2). It does not require any elec-
    tricity or internet connectivity. Its creator - Jesuit Father Jean Bonfa and
    his student had compressed many data into this fresco, including history,
    mathematics, astronomy, and geography. The construction of sundials,
    particularly reflected ones, was evidently a passion for scholars during
    this time. Therefore, it is difficult for ordinary visitors to comprehend all
    of the information of this piece of art. The purpose of this paper is to pres-
    ent all the information about the building in a visual way so readers can
    get a better understanding of it.
    Three parts of the research will be explored: the first looks at the histor-
    ical value and basic principles of astronomy and sundials. In the second
    part, we apply the representation method to analyze the system behind
    this famous reflection sundials. The third part focuses on the possibility
    of using digital technology to present all the information graphically to
    improve the experience of visitors with this historical staircase. In the
    end, we plan to create a digital tool to teach and present about this room.

    1 Mayall, R. Newton, and Mayall W. Margarett, Sundials: Their Construction and Use,
    Cambridge, USA, ed. Sky Publishing Corporation, 1994.
    2 L'Horloge Solaire DV Lycee Stendhal, 1673, Publication of the Foyer du Lycée Stend-

The art of making sundials flourished in the 17th       sundial by Father Emmanuel Maignan, a famous

6.
Master Architecture Thesis 2022/2023
Università Iuav di Venezia

                 physicist and mathematician in Rome; Horologium thamanticum duplex,
                 1662 - The Notebook of Jesuit Ignace-Gaston Pardiès. All of them are out-
                 standing scientific treatises on the sundial of the time.

                 In general, Sundials are recognized as artifacts at the intersection of sev-
                 eral sectors, from science to art, allowing the observer multiple degrees of
                 understanding, ranging from practical information on time measurement.
                 It combines logical geometric approaches, aesthetic quality, and philo-
                 sophical reflection. From the above, the art of Gnomonic requires many
                 practical skills to understand the coordination of vast different operations
                 coexists. Creating sundials, mainly reflecting ones, was a passion among
                 scholars of the period. For example, to determine the exact time repre-
                 sented in Horloge Solaire du lycée Stendhal, readers are requested to do a
                 calculation equivalent to a quiz, meaning they must have a comprehensive
                 understanding of numerous scientific disciplines to appreciate the magnif-
                 icence of this piece of art.

                 It caused the current Horloge Solaire du lycée Stendhal issue, which was
                 opened to the public. Despite its small scale, the mechanical system of this
                 seventeenth-century painting is highly sophisticated, requiring numerous
                 levels of knowledge such as history, geography, astronomy, mathematics,
                 and so on. Despite the tour guide's experience and hard work, it is difficult
                 for guests to comprehend all of the information in one go. This location has
                 become less attractive to visitors who wish to visit Grenoble. Additionally,
                 Because of the covid epidemic, visitors are limited in access, likely escalat-
                 ing the problem. This thesis will examine and display every piece of infor-
                 mation about the building using an architectural representation approach
                 to make this sundial easier to understand for an ordinary visitor.

                 To begin with, understanding the historical value of the reflected sundi-
                 al at Stendhal school, this study will discuss the survey and examination
                 of the sundial in Stendhal, Grenoble, the most famous reflecting sundial
                 described in numerous documents on the 17th century's technological ad-
                 vantage. The second section introduces the survey process and the repre-
                 sentation methodology to analyze the system hidden beneath one of the
                 most well-known reflection sundials - Horloge Solaire du lycée Stendhal.
                 The thesis will create a 3D model file of this place from the information and
                 images obtained during the survey. The comparison method between 3d
                 scanning technology and ideal theories will also be applied to increase the
                 accuracy of this digitalization work.

                 By using representation to explain the complex mechanical system be hide
                 this building, the thesis will illustrate the many layers of information that
                 Father Bonfa and his student had compressed into the fresco. This thesis
                 aims to create a 3d model and make it a tool to represent the information
                 and knowledge that has been compressed within this work for hundreds
                 of years. It will be an educational tool to support guiding visitors to make it
                 easier for them to understand the essence hidden within this theological
                 space.

a ball meant to combat the semi-dark effects. According to various specialists,
the lack of accuracy of most sundials was also observed during this period.2 In
ancient Greece, using a sundial was unusual. Only prosperous cities in ancient
Greece and Rome had sundials that displayed the time: temporal (i.e., irreg-
ular) hours. However, the ancient gnomonic heritage continues in Arab cul-
ture, strengthened by fundamental mathematical principles. Eventually, the
ancient Egyptian and Chaldean civilizations3 developed astronomical studies
to precisely orient massive structures, such as the Pyramids and temples, to
measure time or record seasonal events through shadows (Figure 1.9). These
also supported the development of sundial science.

The concept of splitting the day into 24 hours came from Egypt. Regardless of
the season, the day was divided into 12 equal portions. In addition, the night
was split into 12 similar pieces, a concept that originated in Egypt to simplify
nighttime prayer. This is the concept development 24 hours a day. We have
two parallel time systems for these phenomena: Babylonian hours from sun-
rise onward, and Italic hours from sunset, with variations in other nations 4. In
the following chapter, we will discover and apply those time systems as we
examine the reflecting sundial at Grenoble. Sundials gradually lost their value
for measuring time due to various reasons during the last century, becoming
more of an aesthetic instrument.

                                 once more, and there is increased interest in comprehending the ideas under-
                                 lying this exciting and vital profession. Gnomonics will continue to be a topic
                                 of considerable interest and relevance for future generations.

apart. The celestial sphere is an imaginary sphere surrounding
the Earth, where all celestial objects appear to be (Figure 1.14).
The angle between the hour markers will vary significantly de-
pending on whether the meridian is projected onto a horizontal,
vertical, or inclined plane (Figure 1.15). These timelines intersect
at a point (as projected onto the earth). This is the intersection
of the Sphere's axis with the ground. We have created a horizon-
tal sundial with hour markers, day arcs, and the polar style axis
(Figure 1.16). How accurate can we expect a sundial to be? The
most we can hope for is an accurate one-minute readout if it is
appropriately positioned. On the other hand, the sundial is not
intended to indicate a precise time to the second. Penumbral ef-
fects 5, variances in the temporal equation, and refraction are all
factors that work against an accurate reading.

Sundial Typologies and Classification.                                Figure 1.15 The principle of the sundial. The
                                                                      meridians are projected onto the horizontal
Sundials have been used to track the sun's movement and tell          sundial from the hour lines, and the parallels
time for centuries, and they have been a fascinating area of study    from the diurnal arcs. Sundials: Design Con-
for scientists and enthusiasts alike. Sundials have evolved, each     struction and Use. Springer; Published in As-
with its own set of design and construction requirements. As a        sociation with Praxis 2009, p.38.
result, numerous sundial typologies and classifications have de-
veloped over time. To accurately read the time, the clock needed      Figure 1.17 Mondovie's falling vertical sundi-
to be oriented to the north, accomplished with a compass.             al (Italy). The dial table is oriented vertically.
                                                                      The pattern of hour lines and the position of
Vertical Sundial (Figure 1.17):                                       the style depend on the orientation of the dial.
                                                                      (Photo courtesy of S.Gregori)
Vertical sundials are the most common type, and they can be
found on the walls of older homes, as well as historic buildings,
churches, and monuments. The gnomon is positioned on the
plane of the clock in the direction of the celestial axis, and its
center point represents the center of the celestial sphere.

Equinoctial Ring Sundial (Figure 1.18):

A meridian ring contains: a latitude scale on one side, on the
other side are hour scales, and the bridge has date scales. We can
see the Sun's light displays the time by letting it pass through a
hole at the center and reaching the opposite of the hour ring. It
tells the true solar time anywhere in the world.

Scaphe Sundial - Cup Sundial (Figure 1.19):

This device, known as a "Scaphea," dates back to the Babylonians
and is one of the oldest sundial devices. It is made by projecting
the Hour lines onto a concave hemispherical surface represent-
ing the reverse of the celestial sphere. The gnomon is fixed to the
southern side.

alemmatic sundials are horizontal sundials with a vertical gnomon and hour          Georg Hartman, Nuremberg,
markers positioned in an elliptical pattern. The gnomon is not fixed and must       c. 1525-1564 - Putnam Gal-

Horizontal Sundial (Figure 1.22):                                                   dial, 1548. (Photo courtesy of
                                                                                    Kunsthistorisches Museum
Because it can tell the time at any time, it is the most popular type of sundial.   Vienna )
It may be based on an equatorial dial or created with the help of a bit of trig-
onometry and a pocket calculator. It is simple to install in an open area and
works from sunrise to sunset all year. The horizontal sundial comprises a hor-
izontal table with hour lines marked on it and a style that points to the North
Celestial Pole and casts a shadow on it. When the shadow lies upon one of the
lines, it indicates that hour in solar time.

Sundial In the Camera Obscura (Figure 1.23):

Meridian lines, also known as "camera obscura sundials," are an indoor solar
calendar that only works when the sun is in the south at noon each day, trans-
forming it into a perpetual calendar that indicates the date rather than the
time. Meridian lines are constructed from marble, brass, or bronze strips en-
graved with calendrical, astrological, or geographic data and symbols. These
can be mounted horizontally (on the ground) or vertically (on a wall). An ex-
ample of this sundial is Camera Obscura Sundial at Cava de' Tirreni, designed
by Bovi. Another ancient meridian line no longer functioning can be found in
the monastery of Santa Maria degli Angeli in Pizzofalcone, Naples.

Reflecting (or Catoptric) Dial (Figure 1.24):

The sundial is based on the rules of light-ray reflection. The hour line pattern
is reversed from typical sundials, allowing the sunbeams' reflection angles to
diverge from the surface of a tiny mirror that is attached to a surface, such as a
window sill. Athanasius Kircher described the reflecting dial in the Ars magna
lucis et Umbrae (Rome, 1646) (Figure 1.25). We'll go further into these sundials
and how they were made by analyzing the catoptric dial that is developed by
Father Jean Bonfa in Grenoble, France.

                                                        ters west of Grenoble. It has a beautiful and historic basilica. This
18.                                                     basilica not only has a gorgeous and quite fabulous exterior, but
                                                        it is also the remains of a historic monastery that had belonged
                                                        to the Antonines for seven centuries. It was constructed to pro-
                                                        vide treatment for the victims of the terrible St. Antony's Fire (Er-
                                                        gotism). Antonines must always be alert, regardless of whether
                                                        it is day or night, rain or sun. This is why sundials were invented,
                                                        and King Charles V also ordered a mechanical clock for this place.

                                                        The whole design has 27 steps and covers approximately 25
                                                        square meters. The sundial was painted with colored lines black,
                                                        red, and yellow, indicating Normal, Italic, and Babylonian hours,
                                                        such as in Grenoble (Figure 1.28). This might imply that Bonfa
                                                        created the two. However, no information on the dial's origin is
                                                        available. The sundial is set in the spiraling stairs of a hexago-
                                                        nal tower built against the steeple. Turning left, the stairs receive
                                                        sunlight through several thin vertical slits or loopholes in the
                                                        wall. As the sun moved during the day, a beam of light entered
                                                        the apertures in succession and was reflected on the inner walls,
                                                        steps, and central column of the spiral staircase.

                                      Maignan created another unique Reflective dial
                                      in 1644 at Capo de Ferro in Palazzo Spada. It is
                                      one of the most detailed sundials ever designed

                                      palace was initially owned by Cardinal Spada, a
                                      well-known Maecenas, who requested Maignan
                                      to design a most comprehensive and worldwide
                                      sundial (Figure 1.30). The ceiling between this
                                      wall and the gallery vault has a cylindrical pro-
                                      file. The intersection of the two differently arched
                                      surfaces forms a continuous curve displaying
                                      the dial lines. In the poorly lighted room, it can
                                      only be arduous to distinguish from each other
                                      the lines of local time, Italic, Babylonian and Bib-
                                      lical hours, the zodiac and lines of extreme moon
                                      declination (±28.5°), the Domus coelestis, azi-
                                      muths, almucantars, and hyperbolas for the days
                                      of an even number of daylight hours. My list only
                                      amounts to four dials, but all are inside buildings
                                      and not visible from the streets. There are thou-
                                      sands of cities and villages in Europe - how many
                                      more of these dials may be awaiting discovery?

                                Reflective sundial at Stendhal High School, Grenoble. Let's explore the question:
                                in the many studies of Reflective sundials at the time, which research inspired

                                It is known that Bonfa's work was designed in 1673. During this period, in the
                                great library of the city of Grenoble circulating, three treatises on Reflective

                                Notebook of Jesuit Ignace-Gaston Pardiès. (Figure 1.35). These are maybe the
                                three works that influenced Father Bonfa. We will briefly cover each scholar
                                and their essential treatises to show the influence they have had on this par-
                                ticular discipline.
                                First Influencer: Athanasius Kircher - The first idea about catoptric.
                                Athanasius Kircher (1602 - 1680) 9 was a German Jesuit scholar and polymath
                                who published over 40 essential works in comparative religion, geology, and
                                medicine. Athanasius Kircher mostly gathered his ideas and expertise on
                                Gnomonics in two published works, the Primitiae Gnomonicae Catoptricae
                                (Avignon, 1635) and the Ars Magna Lucis et Umbrae (Rome, 1646). Kircher is
                                considered the pioneer of the Reflective sundial 10. He mentioned the concept
                                of the Reflective sundial in his research paper in the 17th century. In his 1646
                                work "Ars Magna Lucis et Umbrae," Kircher created the phrase "Actinobolis-
                                mus," or "ray throwing." (Figure 1.36). Begin with an introduction to the rules
                                of reflection of flat and curved mirrors, followed by a description of how to
                                build a Reflective sundial. Kirchner includes "Theorems," or reflection laws,
                                such as the angle of incidence and inclination of reflection. The concept is
                                that a reflected picture is inverted left for right and upside down. The idea

Maignan's Perspectiva Horologia is also a complete treatise on the fundamental      Rome, 1648, p. 362.
mathematics of projected geometry, perspective, and optics, including an en-

the development of the Reflective sundial at Stendhal High School in Grenoble.
Third Influencer: Pardiès (Ignace-Gaston) (1636 - 1673)
Ignace-Gaston Pardies was a French Jesuit priest and mechanic. He was born
on September 5, 1636, in Pau (France) and died on April 21, 1674, in Paris. He
is the author of several physics, geometry, and astronomy works. He was also
a pioneer in the wave theory of light. He published " Deux machines propres à
faire les quadrans" in 1687, Paris. In chapter VI, titled " Horologium reflexiones
incubiculo facere" (Figure 1.38) or "Making a reflection clock in a room," we can
read as follows: "The most beautiful quadrans are mirror images. A small mir-
ror is placed over the window, which receives sunlight and reflects a ray of it
into the room so that this ray changes position as the sun advances. Mark all
painted hours in the chamber ...". Many 16th and 17th-century scholars tried to
develop new techniques that simplified the design of sundials using geometric
or computational methodologies (Figure 1.39). Ignace-Gaston Pardies is no dif-
ferent. He designed and explained an instrument that uses a mirror's reflection
to produce his sundial. Although neither as theoretical as Kircher's works nor as
applied to high architectural space as Maignan's, Gaston Pardies' machine is a
one-of-a-kind reference for anyone wishing to use the law of reflection for the
development of a sundial.
Because of the practical aspect of Emmanuel Maignan's "Perspectiva horaria
sive de horographia gnomonica tum theoretica tum practica," it is assumed
that Father Bonfa used the ideal sphere theory discussed in this work to create
his masterpiece Reflective Sundial at Stendhal High School in Grenoble. We'll
go further into this work and try to model an ideal sphere. From there, you
may replicate Father Bonfa's drawings in the Reflective sundial at Stendhal
High School, Grenoble.

This book is organized into four documents containing dozens of
propositions and implications: The first book explains the basic
concepts and theories of classical science; The second book de-
scribes how to practice and illustrates sundials in an easy-to-un-
derstand manner based on the concepts covered in the theo-
ry part of the first book; Catoptrice Horaria is mentioned in the
third book, in this section on the theory of gnomonic catoptrics,
and about the projection of the celestial Sphere onto any sur-
face by using mirror reflections in different shapes, such as con-
cave, spherical, parabolic, convex, cylindrical, and so on; Dioptrice
Horaria is referenced in the fourth book; this book begins with the
basic principles of refraction, its causes, and measurements and
progresses to the theory of the refracting telescope - Horograph-
ia dioptrico gnomonica (Figure 1.42). This section also discusses
optical equipment that has telescopic uses. Through Emanuele
Maignan's legacy, we can identify three profound ideas: the con-
cept of a Celestial Sphere, the projection of a Celestial Sphere, and
the Reflected Celestial Sphere - a Catoptric Sphere. In the next sec-
tion, we will briefly review them to get a broad sense of Maignan.

The first lesson from Emmanuel Maignan's book: The concept of           Figure 1.44 The local Celestial Sphere. The Ce-
the Celestial Sphere                                                    lestial Sphere is referred to when discussing ap-
Comprehension of Maignan's proposal's workings requires a lit-          parent annual motions, while the local Celestial
tle consideration of astronomy: The Celestial Sphere. The celestial     Sphere is used for apparent diurnal and noctur-
Sphere is an imaginary sphere that surrounds the Earth and con-         nal paths, Savoie Denis. Sundials: Design Con-
tains all celestial objects. Our planet seems static to an observer on  struction and Use. Praxis 2009, http://site.ebrary.
the Earth's surface, while the spherical dome of the sky appears to     com/id/10308909 Accessed 15 May 2023.
move around it. It is an essential concept for astronomers and nav-
igators because it lets them quickly indicate the locations of stars,
planets, and other Celestial bodies. Observation of the night sky
will show that the stars seem to travel from east to west around an
imaginary axis that encounters the inner surface of the Celestial
Sphere close to the Pole Star (Figure 1.43). The imaginary axis is
the Earth's rotational axis, passing through the Celestial Sphere at
two opposite points, the North and South Celestial Pole. By analo-
gy with the Earth, we also define a Celestial Equator. We get the lo-
cal observer's Celestial Sphere by correlating the Celestial Sphere
with the observer's latitude, which is the Sphere that Maignan
used to build the reflected sundial. We can define a local Celestial
Sphere at any point on the Earth's surface (Figure 1.44).

Astrological       The zero point for celestial Longitude (for right ascension) is the Vernal
Houses of The      Equinox, the intersection of the Ecliptic and the celestial Equator near
Sky (Optional)     the Sun in the Northern Hemisphere Spring. The point on the Ecliptic
                   where the Sun is most north of the celestial Equator is termed the
                   Summer Solstice, and the point where it is most south of the celestial
                   Equator is termed the Winter Solstice. The other meeting of the Celes-
                   tial Equator and the Ecliptic is the Autumnal Equinox.

                   The zodiacal constellations are found in a ring along the celestial
                   Equator, formed from the chance superposition of the stars which lie
                   along these lines of sight. As the Earth rotates once around the Sun in
                   a year, the Sun appears to pass through (or lie in front of ) each of these
                   constellations in turn. Its path is called the Ecliptic. Here is the list of
                   Zodiac: Gemini (the Twins), Cancer (the Crab), Leo (the Lion), Virgo
                   (the Maiden), Libra (the Scale), Scorpio (the Scorpion), Sagittarius (the
                   Archer), Capricorn (the Goat), Aquarius (the Water-Carrier), Pisces (the
                   Fish), Aries (the Ram), and Taurus (the Bull).

                   Latitude and Longitude are the units that represent the coordinates in
                   the geographic coordinate system. The geographic coordinate system
                   is a spherical or ellipsoidal coordinate system for measuring and com-
                   municating positions directly on the Earth as latitude and Longitude.

                   The astronomical hour system is also known as the French hour
                   system (civil time or equal time). It tells the solar time as we are using
                   today on modern watches. It provides twenty-four fixed equal periods
                   of the day and is divided into two cycles every twelve hours.

                   Babylonian hours are equinoctial hours counted from sunrise. The
                   interval between two sunrises is divided into 24 equal hours.

                   Italian hours are equinoctial hours counted from the last sunset. The
                   interval between two sunsets is divided into 24 equal hours.

                   The twelve houses are purely mathematical creations. The unit circle,
                   which is 360 degrees in diameter, can represent the expanse of the
                   heavens.

Rome, 1648, p. 163.              The problem is clearly explained on page 283 of Maignan's treatise dedicated
                                 to catoptric sundials, emblematically entitled Perspectiva horaria sive de Horo-
                                 graphia gnomonica (Maignan year 1648). Segment OB creates the celestial
                                 Sphere into two equal versions; the left half features a schematization of the
                                 Sun on the arch of the sky vault and the outermost lines of a luminous cone,
                                 i.e., the incident sun rays. The right half of the image displays the Sun and the
                                 luminous cone in symmetrical positions, corresponding to the left half. A plane
                                 is placed between the mirror, represented by the ellipse VX, and the symmetri-
                                 cal representation of the Sun; it intercepts the reflection of the luminous cone,
                                 forming another picture of the celestial body on its surface (Figure 1.48). If we
                                 compare Maignan's scheme to the principles of perspective, the two suns can
                                 be intended as the bases of the visual cones whose vertices are in the point
                                 of observation D and E. As a result, he created a reflected version of a celestial
                                 sphere shown in (Figure 1.49). In the following chapter; we will use this version
                                 of a celestial sphere to analyze the reflected sundial case studies.

Maignan's celestial projection technique:                          Figure 1.56 3D Reconstruction Figure 1.55 3D Reconstruction
To build and trace the lines of the systems present
on the sundial, Maignan used various tools he in-
vented. These instruments are illustrated in detail
in his book Perspedtiva horaria and the building
instructions. Father Maignan used this method to
indicate a catoptric sphere. Firstly, locating the local
latitude (Figure 1.50), i.e., which axis goes through
the North Star. We can identify the Sun's declina-
tions and astronomical hours, Italic and Babylonian.
Follow up by drawing the meridian line from north
to south and the horizon line. With the "Vertica-
le mobile" tool (Figure 1.51), it is possible to create
the celestial coordinates. Using horizontal sundial
and vertical style, both are located on the mirror's
position; we can create a first draft for the sundial
(Figure 1.52) by projecting them onto the ceiling
and walls of the interior. With the "Meridiano mo-
bile" tool (Figure 1.53), It is possible to create lines of
the hourly astronomical system. The same principle
will be applied to indicate the Italic and Babylonian
dial system (Figure 1.54). Finally, the temporary hour
lines are drawn to intersect with all the others on
the equinoctial line. The system Of the Astrological
Houses of The Sky will be drawn with the "Verticale
mobile" tool positioned horizontally and aligned on
the north-south line.

                    5.

           Case Studies on Notable
               Reflective Sundial

                                  After modeling the catoptric or celestial sphere based on Emanuele Maignan's
                                  research studies, we will apply it to analyze some typical examples of historical
                                  reflective sundials. The objective was to examine the applicability of Emanu-
                                  ele Maignan's research method. There are two candidates for the case studies
                                  analyzing: the reflective sundial at Palazzo Spada, Rome - Emmanuel Maignan
                                  (1644) and the reflective sundial at The Church of San Cristo in Brescia. These
                                  reflective sundials were created on complex surfaces such as walls and vaults.
                                  The drawn lines are indicated based on the luminous dot over months (or even
                                  years). Creating a sundial on any surface becomes a geometric projection prob-
                                  lem of a celestial sphere onto a particular surface.

                                  The Geometric Problem of The Reflecting Sundial in Palazzo Spada.
                                  The sundial is located inside a gallery with a mirror on the wall facing approx-
                                  imately southeast. Maignan made this place becomes a masterpiece of com-
                                  plication! The vaulted ceiling of the gallery has a cylindrical profile, and the in-
                                  tersection of the two differently arched surfaces forms a continuous curve on
                                  which the dial lines are drawn. As usual, the various line systems presented dif-
                                  ferent colors. Indeed, the creation is simply the projection of the hour lines on
                                  a cylinder vault. The difficulty to be solved is projecting onto surfaces of any
                                  shape and orientation from a given point in space (Figure 1.59). Considering the
                                  specific case of Maignan's catoptric sundials, the question can be reformulated
                                  in geometrical terms as the intersection between two quadratic surfaces:

Figure 1.63 The vertical plane      cloisters. The Church is usually defined as the Sistine Chapel of Brescia, given the
clock with French hours kept in     scenic affinities of Michelangelo's work in the Roman Sistine Chapel. The creator
the cloister of Santa Maria della   of this exquisite reflective sundial remains unknown. We can only guess that
Pace in Alzano Lombardo near        it was Fra Domenico, a student of Franciscan Father Vincenzo Coronelli. In the
Bergamo, 2011.                      region of Bergamo, there are a huge collection of sundials (Figure 1.62,63,64).
                                    Some are in terrible condition, while others have just been repaired, but only for
                                    the visual aspect. Therefore, they are mostly inactive due to the significant diffi-
                                    culties in precisely situating the gnomonic mirror. Only a few sundials still retain
                                    their fine skill of chronometry, as the one in the Church of San Cristo, Brescia.
                                    The concept of operation of The Reflecting Sundial in Brescia is the same as else-
                                    where. It employs the use of a reflecting mirror to send sunlight deep into the
                                    architectural environment. The mirror was placed in the center of the arch. The
                                    mirror is 25 cm from the ceiling. Reflective glass and stainless-steel panels were
                                    modified to suit the outdoor conditions during the 2002 renovations (Figure
                                    1.65). On the decorative ceiling, one can read the hours of the day, the months,
                                    the zodiac signs, and Latin inscriptions honoring the sun and moon. The design
                                    presents the Italics Hour in gray with 24 hours as in the clock, after which red
                                    French hours are added to the time of Napoleon, counted from 1 to 12. The lines
                                    of the hours intersect with the lines of the MONTHs. Furthermore, the month
                                    at the top of the sign is June (summer solstice), and the month at the bottom
                                    is December (winter solstice). Each month is represented by a zodiac sign. The
                                    12-hour meridian with the name of the city of Brescia is placed in the middle
                                    when the sun is at its highest point of the day. We can also observe the time
                                    in different locations such as the Canary Islands, Jerusalem, Mecca, Lisbon, the
                                    East Indies, etc. The popularity of the reflective sundial most likely indicates a
                                    hidden spiritual meaning underneath the religion's soul. The dome's ceiling is
                                    symbolic of Creation, and the dome itself is a portrayal of the Dome of Heaven.20
                                    Where the reflection of the sun in a faint light produces the sensation that Love
                                    originates from God 21. We reconstructed an ideal celestial sphere associated to
                                    Brescia's latitude (45.5416° N). We are using the same operations of reflection
                                    and intersection of the luminous surfaces with the 3D model of the dome. By
                                    applying a 3D Simulation of the Celestial Sphere to Analysis, we get the inter-
                                    section of the geometric of the sun's light one by one with the corridor's dome

                    1.

      A History of The Lycée Stendhal

                                 (Grenoble,France)

100km                    A History of The Lycée Stendhal in Grenoble (Figure 2.1)
                         The Lycée Stendhal, located in the southern French city of Grenoble, is a
       France            prominent secondary school with a long history of over two centuries. The
                         Cité Scolaire Stendhal (college and high school) is a public secondary school
       Grenoble          located on Place Jean Achard in Grenoble. A fantastic position in the center
                         of Grenoble, with easy access to public transportation, culture, and sports
                         facilities. The school's history dates back to the 17th century. Lycée Stendhal
                         was part of the former Collègedes Jésuites. It is the oldest school in Greno-
                         ble. Its pupils have included Stendhal (after whom it is now named)22 (Figure
                         2.2). The school's history is tidily connected with the Society of Jesus devel-
                         opment in Grenoble since its first member settled here in January 1623.23 By
                         authorization of Louis XIII, a branch of the Society of Jesus was established
                         in Grenoble (passing through the capital Dauphiné). Bishop Scarron super-
                         vised the entire process. The Society of Jesus was also located in Chambéry,
                         Lyon, Tournon, Vienne, etc.

The Invisible Gem of Horloge Solaire Du Lycée Stendhal
Grenoble (FR)

                    3.

           Methods of Survey and
           Digital Reconstruction

Figure 2.9 Photos of guests    Grenoble, a beautiful mountain city in France, hosts a hidden architectural
visiting Grenoble's reflected  wonder: the Reflective Sundial at The Lycée Stendhal. This unique structure,
sundial on March 26th, 2022.   rich in history and mystery, has piqued the interest of both residents and tour-
(Photo courtesy of Ho Trong    ists. In this part, the thesis presents survey proposals for the Sundial, provid-
Nhan)                          ing an insight into its unique aspects and the problems encountered through-
                               out the research.
38.                            Our journey begins with a desire to learn, which started before September
                               2021. Seeking to delve into the secrets of Grenoble's rich heritage, a letter was
                               addressed to the city's tourist office, which expressed a desire to conduct a
                               brief survey at the reflective Sundial of Lycée Stendhal. However, time passed,
                               and despite the bureau's guarantee that the question would be forwarded to
                               the relevant department, the hunger for information remained unsatisfied.
                               Despite the absence of an official answer, a decision was made to seize the op-
                               portunity and explore the Reflective Sundial as a tourist with a strong interest
                               in Grenoble's history.
                               Therefore, this thesis might be a reference guide for anyone seeking a spon-
                               taneous experience. The primary goal is to gain a 3D scan that captures the
                               character of historic structures, which is motivated by ambition and curiosity.
                               The crowded atmosphere of the site of tourism provided a challenge to over-
                               come.
                               The sundial survey took place on February 26th and March 26th, 2022.28 The
                               investigation required me to measure the staircase's size, photograph its walls
                               and ceilings, and carefully document every aspect. To ensure a comprehen-
                               sive survey, specific requirements must be followed to capture the character
                               of The Lycée Stendhal's Reflective Sundial. The conditions needed are as fol-
                               lows:
                               · Time Restriction: It is critical to complete the survey within one hour, which

                                   is the time limit for visiting purposes.
                               · Considerate Exploration: It is critical to explore without disturbing other

                                   visitors' interest in the building's history (Figure 2.9).
                               · Staircase Geometry: The survey must adapt to the specific elements of the

                                   staircase, including the stepping patterns and the U-Shape design.
                               · Attention to Detail: Due to the limited access to the Reflective Sundial, it is

                                   critical to precisely document every minute detail throughout the survey.
                               To commence the survey, the first step involves measuring the essential di-
                               mensions of the staircase, including the number of steps and the width and
                               height of the area. This fundamental data offers a foundation for future re-
                               search. In addition to measurements, the photographs acquired during the
                               survey will help digitally reconstruct the Reflective Sundial.
Master Architecture Thesis 2022/2023
Università Iuav di Venezia

Method of Dimension Measurement                          Figure 2.10 Dimensions developed from the survey at Greno-
Initially, the passageway of the Reflective Sundial      ble's reflecting sundial.
was measured using a metric string as the staircase
took a U-shaped form incorporating a landing for en-     Figure 2.11 Dimensions obtained by the trilateration meth-
hanced safety. To ensure measurement accuracy, two       od.
parallel stages were employed for calibration and im-
provement.
The first technique involved utilizing a ruler to mea-
sure the staircase's specific details and overall di-
mensions. Measuring tape or a laser measure was
effective due to the limited space of approximately
100m2. The following steps were followed for the
measurement process:
1. Calculate the total number of stairs and treads in

    the staircase. Each side consisted of 15 stairs and
    one landing (Figure 2.10).
2. As the accompanying image depicts, a typical
    step has a riser height of 160mm, a 380mm tread
    depth, and a 2050mm tread width (Figure 2.10).
3. A measuring tape was used to collect measure-
    ments from the third floor's finished level to the
    landing step and from the landing step to the sec-
    ond floor's finished level. The dimensions mea-
    sured for each side were around 2.5m, with an
    average ceiling height of 4300mm (Figure 2.10).
The second method involved using trilateration to
calculate the overall length of the staircase.29 The
measuring procedure was divided into two sections
to minimize interruption to other guests during the
tour guide's presentation. While the tour guide was
presenting on the East side of the staircase, the mea-
surements occurred from the staircase's West side
opposite. This procedure is similar for the other side.
This guarantees that the survey did not interfere with
the people around.
The trilateration method was implemented to mea-
sure the stair, establishing eight fixed stations to
"spot" as many points as possible, ensuring survey
accuracy. Coordinating points A, B, G, and H were lo-
cated 2500mm higher from the finished level, while
corresponding points D, E, F, and C were situated at
the same level as the landing (Figure 2.11). After the
measuring procedure was completed, two copies of
the staircase size were acquired, one for each mea-
surement technique. Overlapping these versions
produced a final file with precise dimensions (Figure
2.12).

                                                         Figure 2.12 The 3D model has been generated by comparing
                                                         the two measuring methods.

grammetry.                                              Photography techniques must also follow the same

40.                                                     visitors. In this session, we'll divide the stairs into 3
                                                        phases to maximize later photography and photo-
                                                        grammetry work. It is divided into two sections for
                                                        the stair sides and one for the landing. We will cap-

                                                        and ceiling. The photographic survey was carried out
                                                        by choosing holding points at 600mm, 1200mm, and
                                                        1800mm from the ground from which images were
                                                        shot to generate the spherical panoramas (Figure
                                                        2.13).
                                                        The photographing procedure is shown in the
                                                        cross-section below. The aim is to cover the stairs with
                                                        pictures that overlap approximately 60% of the area.
                                                        In addition, photographs were taken orthogonally to
                                                        the ceiling along the corridor's central longitudinal
                                                        axis. In total, 309 photos were taken with at least 60%
                                                        overlap between one and the other, producing ten
                                                        hemispherical panoramas (Figure 2.14).

Translation of Latin Inscriptions
When visitors enter Grenoble's Reflective Sundial, they are surrounded by an immersive setting filled with nu-
merous Latin writings carefully created by Father Bonfa. These inscriptions serve as detailed elucidations of the
astronomical elements encompassed within this splendid masterpiece. By undertaking a comprehensive study
of these Latin inscriptions, an enhanced comprehension of the sundial's functioning and utilization can be at-
tained (Figure 2.30).

The Projection of The System of Declinations of The Sun - The                                                                                                                               Figure 2.40 The intersection between the sys-
Signs of The Zodiac of Celstial Sphere on The Interior Space                                                                                                                                tem of declinations of the sun with the staircase.
                                                                                                                                                                                            Orange lines: System of declinations of the sun
The System of Declinations of the Sun can be identified and rep-                                                                                                                            generated by the ideal celestial.
resented on a celestial sphere by depicting orbitals resembling                                                                                                                             Orange Surface: The conic surface of light with-
light cones. This system comprises a collection of cones that share                                                                                                                         in a year.
a common vertex and axis of rotation, horizontally inclined at an
angle of  with respect to the north. The value of  corresponds to
the latitude of Grenoble, specifically  = 45.1885°.

The plane traversing the equator is designated as the 0° point to
establish a reference origin (Figure 2.38). Subsequently, the cones
extend along arcs inclined to the equator at various angles, name-
ly +/-11.4°, +/-20°, and +/-23°27' (representing the tropical/solstice
positions), as outlined in the Figure 2.39. These angles correspond
to the relatively constant declination values of the Sun at specific
locations throughout the year. Notably, these four distinct eclip-
tic points and the corresponding periods when the Sun occupies
these positions are commonly referred to as the equinoxes and
solstices. Thus, along the surface of the celestial sphere, the Sun
moves in day and year orbits. The Sun, in its motion on the eclip-
tic, passes through the constellations known as the zodiac signs,
which together form the Zodiac (Figure 2.40). The lines of the Sun's
declinations inside the reflective sundial result from the intersec-
tion of the room surfaces with the light cones (Figure 2.41).

                                     The Geometrical Relationship Is Employed to Generate the Declina-
                                         tions of The Sun Inside the Inner Space of a Reflected Sundial.

The System of Declinations of the Sun can be identified by light cones. The plane at the equator is the 0° point - reference origin. The
other cones inclined to the equator at: 11, 20, and 23 degrees. The lines of the Sun's declinations inside the reflective sundial are the
intersection of the room surfaces with the light cones.

              French Hours Dials

Figure 2.42 The inscription decorated Roman                                                                  Introduction To the French Hour System:
numerals inside the reflective sundial in Greno-
ble.                                                                                                         The astronomical hour system is also known as the French hour
                                                                                                             system (civil time or equal time). It tells the solar time as we are us-
                                                                                                        M.G  ing today on modern watches. The term "Astronomical" originates
                                                                                                             from the application of the hourly notation similar to the notation
               I   2                                                                                         used by astronomers. It provides twenty-four fixed equal periods
          M.G            1 3 12                                                                              of the day and is divided into two cycles every twelve hours. The
                          M.G                                                                                French hour system consists of 24 hours a day starting at mid-
              XII                                                                                            night, used in France around 1500. They are called the French hour
                   3             12                                                                          system to distinguish it from the Italic time adopted by Italy and
I                                                                                                            other countries.
                   4 M.G             11
                                                                                                             The Existing Condition of The Sundial in Grenoble
                                         10
                                                                                                             The set of hour markers are curves painted "mixto colore", i.e. in a
                             III IV 9                                                                        dark gray or "Paris mud", according to Abbé Gattel.33 Thinner lines
                   II                                                                                        indicate the half-hours make obtaining more precision in read-
                                                                                                             ing the time possible. The numbering is in Roman numerals (VIII,
                                                                             8                               IX, X, XI, XII for the morning hours and I, II, III, IV for the evening
                                                                                                             hours) and Arabic (Figure 2.42). These lines represent the direction
                                 X IX VIII                                                                   of movement of the sun's bright spot on the same day, especially
                                                                                                             during the winter and summer solstices, as well as at the time of
M.G XII XI                                                                                                   the equinoxes.

system of the celestial sphere on the interior                                                               wall to the east of the second step represents evening time. The
space during the period 12:00 in a day.                                                                      small wall in the middle shows contrasting solar time/moon time

Figure 2.44 Orthogonal Projections of French                                                                 mon dial. The position of the bright spot between the two near-
                                                                                                             est lines is evaluated relatively intuitively. Whereas with a regular
Hours Dials.                                                                                                 dial, the hours from VI in the morning to XII will go downward.
                                                                                                             Then here, due to the reflection, on the contrary, these hours go
58.                                                                                                          up again. The XII line or meridian indicating the location of the
                                                                                                             Grenoble meridian has been carefully drawn. It is used to place the
                                                                                                             dials and clocks.

                                                                                                             The Projection of The Hour Angle of Celestial Sphere on The In-
                                                                                                             terior Space

                                                                                                             An ideal celestial sphere has been constructed, specifically de-
                                                                                                             signed to correspond to the latitude of Grenoble. This celestial
                                                                                                             sphere incorporates a network of hour lines known as French hour
                                                                                                             lines, formed through the intersection of the celestial sphere and
                                                                                                             planes of light (Figure 2.43). These planes of light are generated
                                                                                                             by the Sun and the North Star's axis. It is important to note that
                                                                                                             the tilt angle of the north pole axis varies following the specific
                                                                                                             latitude of the sundial's location. In the case of Grenoble, this tilt
                                                                                                             angle is set at 45.1885°, aligning with its corresponding latitude (
                                                                                                             = 45.1885°) (Figure 2.44). The celestial sphere's astronomical hour
                                                                                                             system is identified by applying a fundamental astronomical pa-

French hour lines, formed through the intersection of the celestial sphere and planes of light. These light planes are then rotated around
the north pole in multiples of 15 degrees, starting from the meridian plane that represents 12:00. The astronomical hour lines, also
known as French hours, are established through the intersection between the interior surfaces and the planes of light.

               Italian Hours Dials

Figure 2.58 Orthogonal Projections of Italian     Introduction to the Italian time system:
Hours Dials
                                                  It was not the hours of ancient Rome used for the Christian lit-
                                                  urgy, which varied according to the seasons. Italian hours are
                                                  equinoctial hours counted from the last sunset. The Italian hour
                                                  is 0:00 at sunset. The interval between two sunsets is divided
                                                  into 24 equal hours in-scribed in Arabic numerals on the dial and
                                                  called Italic hours in treatises on gnomonic (the art of building
                                                  sundials). In the mid-1300s, the Italian time system was used.
                                                  It specifies the number of hours until sunset (Hour of Vespers):
                                                  for the benefit of public life, knowing the hours of light that re-
                                                  mained to end a working day was essential so that one would
                                                  not be left in the dark outside the town or on the streets, which
                                                  were not very pleasant at the time. The Italian time system was
                                                  widespread, particularly in the peninsula and Catholic nations,
                                                  and it was used until the second half of the eighteenth century
                                                  when the modern system superseded it. Today, it is mainly pre-
                                                  served in the repair of antique dials.

                                                  The existing condition of the sundial in Grenoble

                                                   In the context of the sundial in Grenoble, Father Bonfa imple-
                                                      mented a distinct inscription, "ROMANE RVBRAM" (red for
                                                          Rome) (Figure 2.58), symbolizing the utilization of Italian
                                                             hour lines. These hour lines exhibit a symmetrical con-
                                                                 struction, commencing from sunset (Figure 2.59).
                                                                    They are also great circles obtained by rotating
                                                                        the horizon plane around the polar axis. The
                                                                            coexistence of the Italian and Babylonian
                                                                               hour systems within the sundial com-
                                                                                   ple-ments one another, enabling the
                                                                                      determination of post-sunset and
                                                                                          post-sunrise hour counts. Re-

         Twelve Astrological Houses
            - The Celestial Houses

Figure 2.66 Orthogonal Projections of Twelve       Introduction to Twelve Astrological Houses:
Astrological Houses
                                                   The Twelve Astrological Houses, also known as the Celestial Hous-
                                                   es, represent a fundamental concept within astrology. These
                                                   houses are mathematical divisions, each spanning 30 degrees.
                                                   The celestial sphere, with its unit circle of 360 degrees, symbolical-
                                                   ly represents the expansive heavens. Astrologers construct horo-
                                                   scopes by considering the positions of celestial bodies, primarily
                                                   in two distinct contexts. Firstly, they analyze the locations of these
                                                   bodies within the various signs of the Zodiac, often referred to as
                                                   the Celestial Houses of the Sun. Secondly, they observe the posi-
                                                   tions of celestial bodies with the horizon of a specific geographic
                                                   location, known as the celestial Houses relative to the horizon.

                                                   During the late 17th century, astrology remained a subject of
                                                   study and exploration. As previously mentioned, the author of The
                                                   Sundial referred to the positions of the zodiac signs. In astrology,
                                                   the celestial sphere is divided into twelve distinct houses, each
                                                   corresponding to a two-hour solar time interval.

                                                    The existing condition of the sundial in Grenoble

                                                         The sundial in Grenoble, as previously described by Father
                                                            Bonfa, features inscriptions indicating the positions of
                                                                the celestial Houses along the walls and ceilings of the
                                                                   staircase (Figure 2.65).

                                                                        These inscriptions provide valuable informa-
                                                                            tion regarding the alignment of the celestial
                                                                               Houses with the Grenoble horizon at spe-
                                                                                   cific times throughout the day (Figure
                                                                                      2.66). We read the following inscrip-
                                                                                          tions on Plate 2, West wall, on the
                                                                                             left DOMVS COELI nearly 8 am
                                                                                                 going up.

termining the Celestial Houses resembles the establishment of the
astronomical hour system, such as the French hour system. The
planes of light utilized to define the Celestial Houses are derived
from the combined influence of the Sun and the axis of the North
Star (Figure 2.69). They correspond to the celestial Houses. The lines
of this system arise from a bundle of planes with an angle of 30 de-
grees and the axis of rotation towards the North Star, i.e., north (Fig-
ure 2.70). They are tilted relative to the horizon 45.1885 degrees,
equal to Grenoble's latitude  = 45.1885 degrees. By employing
this method, the projection of the Celestial Houses onto the celes-
tial sphere enables a comprehensive understanding of their spatial
arrangement within the interior space (Figure 2.71).

                                     The Geometrical Relationship Is Employed to Generate the Celestial
                                              Houses Inside the Inner Space of a Reflected Sundial.

The Twelve Astrological Houses, also known as the Celestial Houses, represent a fundamental concept within astrology. The projection
of the Celestial Houses onto the interior space can be achieved by delineating their presence on a celestial sphere. The generation of
these houses involves using a series of planes, each characterized by a rotation angle of 30° and a rotation axis aligned towards the north
pole.

                    6.

   Conclusion: Comparison of the Ideal
  Sphere Model to the 3D Survey Model

The accuracy of complex information systems within the reflecting sun-
dial at Stendhal High School in Grenoble was investigated and evaluated
through a comparative analysis of the photogrammetry-based 3D recon-
struction and the intersection lines obtained from projecting the celestial
sphere into the interior space. This examination allows for an in-depth
assessment of the study's precision. The 3D model represents the current
state of the reflecting sundial (Figure 2.72). At the same time, the projec-
tion of the celestial sphere into the interior space signifies the envisioned
ideal outcome envisioned by Father Bonfa (Figure 2.73).

By overlaying these two components, precise and congruent results are
obtained. The figure below illustrates a detailed comparison between
the sundial's current condition and the ideal lines made by the 3d stim-
ulation (Figure 2.74). A remarkable relative precision exists between the
lines drawn by Father Bonfa in the 17th century and the lines derived
from the 3D model. Nevertheless, it is essential to acknowledge the lim-
itations imposed by the existing condition of the sundial in Grenoble.
Due to the upward geometry of the stairway, certain areas of the ceiling
became inaccessible for the beams of light at specific times of the year,
preventing Father Bonfa from accurately depicting them.

The precision achieved can be attributed to Father Bonfa's use of a tri-         Figure 2.72 The 3D reconstruction of the
al-and-error approach during the design process. The edited lines on this        reflective sundial in Grenoble.
wall could still be visible to the naked eye after a hundred years in less
than 100 square meters.

This demonstrates that the author of this dial invested his time in cal-
culating methods. This work is equivalent to an efficient scientific study,
and the author has always prioritized correctness. This article proves Je-
suit Father Jean Bonfa's skill in mathematics and dedication.

However, it is essential to note that this thesis only provides an imper-        Figure 2.73 The Reconstruction of The lo-
fect summary considering the depth of knowledge surrounding this                 cal Celestial Sphere.
historical site. Numerous untold stories exist regarding the formation of
the spreadsheets, which were implicitly recognized for their correctness
throughout the research. Throughout the study, the thesis implicitly rec-
ognized their correctness. Furthermore, the historical stories behind Bon-
fa's father's creation of this masterwork are an area that requires further
investigation. It is believed that it will serve as a source of inspiration for
future research and provide a greater understanding of the guiding con-
cepts of its clever and erudite creator.

                              Digital Version of Reflective Sundial in Grenoble, France.

                              The focus in the final part of this thesis is on using the
                              potential of digital technology to improve visitors' experi-
                              ences with the historical staircase. We have created a com-
                              prehensive digital version as an engaging and exciting
                              instrument for teaching and displaying the sundial.
                              The complete 3D model is now available online, allowing
                              visitors to dive further into the staircase's historical impor-
                              tance. By leveraging this platform, we exhibit our inven-
                              tion and serve as an excellent resource for people inter-
                              ested in learning more about this outstanding reflective
                              sundial in Grenoble, France.
                              We can considerably improve the overall experience for
                              individuals who come to tour the ancient staircase by just
                              scanning the QR code provided below.

Graphic by Ho Trong Nhan              85.
The sun clock at Grenoble is a testament to the technological advances of the 17th cen-
tury. This clock is built in fresco and covers 100m2 of wall and ceiling space. It does not
require any electricity or internet connectivity. Its creator - Jesuit Father Jean Bonfa and
his student had compressed many data into this fresco, including history, mathematics,
astronomy, and geography. The construction of sundials, particularly reflected ones, was
evidently a passion for scholars during this time. Therefore, it is difficult for ordinary visi-
tors to comprehend all of the information of this piece of art. The purpose of this paper is
to present all the information about the building in a visual way so readers can get a better
understanding of it.

System Of the      of the Ecliptic and the celestial Equator near the Sun in the Northern Hemisphere Spring. The
Astrological       point on the Ecliptic where the Sun is most north of the celestial Equator is termed the Summer
Houses of The      Solstice, and the point where it is most south of the celestial Equator is termed the Winter Sol-
Sky (Optional)     stice. The other meeting of the Celestial Equator and the Ecliptic is the Autumnal Equinox.

                   The zodiacal constellations are found in a ring along the celestial Equator, formed from the
                   chance superposition of the stars which lie along these lines of sight. As the Earth rotates once
                   around the Sun in a year, the Sun appears to pass through (or lie in front of ) each of these
                   constellations in turn. Its path is called the Ecliptic. Here is the list of Zodiac: Gemini (the Twins),
                   Cancer (the Crab), Leo (the Lion), Virgo (the Maiden), Libra (the Scale), Scorpio (the Scorpion),
                   Sagittarius (the Archer), Capricorn (the Goat), Aquarius (the Water-Carrier), Pisces (the Fish), Aries
                   (the Ram), and Taurus (the Bull).

                   Latitude and Longitude are the units that represent the coordinates in the geographic coordi-
                   nate system. The geographic coordinate system is a spherical or ellipsoidal coordinate system for
                   measuring and communicating positions directly on the Earth as latitude and Longitude.

                   The astronomical hour system is also known as the French hour system (civil time or equal time).
                   It tells the solar time as we are using today on modern watches. It provides twenty-four fixed
                   equal periods of the day and is divided into two cycles every twelve hours.

                   Babylonian hours are equinoctial hours counted from sunrise. The interval between two sunrises
                   is divided into 24 equal hours.

                   Italian hours are equinoctial hours counted from the last sunset. The interval between two sun-
                   sets is divided into 24 equal hours.

                   The twelve houses are purely mathematical creations. The unit circle, which is 360 degrees in
                   diameter, can represent the expanse of the heavens.

The reflection                                                                                                                                                                                              and science two and a half centuries after its creation.
of the sun's rays onto a mirror positioned on the windowsill
creates this light spot. Father Jean Bonfa's work is around one
hundred square meters in size, including many different time
systems inside it: French time, i.e., the current time, counting
from midnight; Babylonian time, starting at sunrise; Italian
time, which he called Roman time; twelve months of the solar
calendar, the twelve signs of the zodiac, which are represented
by symbolic images, and the four seasons of the year: AESTAS,
AUTUMNUS, HIBERNUM, and VER.
The clock also shows the times of sunrise and sunset. By the
sundial (La Méridienne ), we can acknown the season, month,
and day by the position of the bright spot. At the same time,
the sundial includes lunar calendar information and deter-
mines the moon's age relative to the sun.

                                                                                                      The Lycée Stendhal, located in the southern French city of Grenoble, is a prominent secondary school with a long history of over two centu-
                                                                                                      ries. The Cité Scolaire Stendhal (college and high school) is a public secondary school located on Place Jean Achard in Grenoble. A fantastic
                                                                                                      position in the center of Grenoble, with easy access to public transportation, culture, and sports facilities. The school's history dates back
                                                                                                      to the 17th century. Lycée Stendhal was part of the former Collègedes Jésuites. It is the oldest school in Grenoble. The school's history is
                                                                                                      tidily connected with the Society of Jesus development in Grenoble since its first member settled here in January 1623. By authorization of
                                                                                                      Louis XIII, a branch of the Society of Jesus was established in Grenoble (passing through the capital Dauphiné). Bishop Scarron supervised
                                                                                                      the entire process. The Society of Jesus was also located in Chambéry, Lyon, Tournon, Vienne, etc. Before the entire building was finished
                                                                                                      in 1703, they constructed the surrounding buildings gradually.

An inscription on the sun-
dial reads as follows:
 "GALLE NIGRAM ROMANE

   RVBRAM FVLVAM QVE
   BABELE HIC TIBI PINGO
 HORAM MIXTO DO SIGNA

          COLORE"
   Translated, it conveys:
  "I paint for you here the
French time in black, Roman
time in red and Babylonian
time in yellow. I provide you
with the signs of the zodiac

       in mixed colors."

                                                        sure a comprehen-sive survey, specific requirements must be followed to capture the character of The Lycée
                                                        Stendhal's Reflective Sundial.
